Yeah, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, like I might be sitting there looking calm, but inside it feels like this pressure building up—like I need to move, or switch to something else, or at least have something in my hands.

Yeah, I think stress probably makes it louder, but the restlessness is kind of there even when I’m not, like, actively upset. I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside there’s this pressure, like I need to move or switch tasks or have something in my hands.

Sorry, I’m thinking of this one meeting where I had a pen and kept clicking it until someone looked at me, and then I felt ridiculous and stopped, but then I just started bouncing my foot instead. So… yes, stress doesn’t help, but even on normal days, sitting still can feel like I’m trying not to crawl out of my skin.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally made me come in is this thing where I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. I’ll tell myself, “Just stay put,” and then two minutes later I’m wandering off for some random reason. And then I’ll start thinking about, I don’t know, the laundry or some random thing I forgot to check, and suddenly moving seems like the only way to make that feeling stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what you're doing when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. catch myself answering teammates before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question, and sometimes I’m already talking when I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting my turn in stand-up or sitting on hold with the bank makes me weirdly irritated, and I’ll keep checking the time or jump in too fast even though I know it comes off as impatient.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset And I’d rush through work even if I actually understood it, which is funny because now I’m a software developer and rushing is, like, the worst possible habit when you’re trying not to break production—sorry, tangent.\n\nBut yes, as a kid, there were definitely comments about me not staying seated and kind of blurting things out or hurrying through stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to focus affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, a bit. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve completely stopped doing anything, exactly. It’s more like I’ve had to change how I handle certain day-to-day stuff, especially planning things.

Like at work, if there’s coordination or scheduling or some little follow-up task, people end up checking in with me more than they used to. And at home too, with family plans or organizing something, I’ll have good intentions and then someone has to remind me, and it creates friction.

I really hate that feeling, because I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks. Like, not the person you want in charge of the details, which is… not a great feeling.

Yeah, even with things I want to do. We’ve been dealing with mortgage documents, and I’ll start reading them because, obviously, that’s important, and then I notice I’ve read the same paragraph four times and none of it has gone in. And then I get irritated with myself, because it’s not that I don’t care. My brain just sort of… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus consistently, regardless of the time of day or the activity you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not perfectly predictable by time of day, I don’t think. It kind of shows up across stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like your restlessness and inability to focus have caused significant problems in your personal or professional life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / software_developer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It has started creating friction at work and at home because people have to follow up with me more than they used to, and I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks even though I’m trying hard.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, “significant” sounds big, and it’s not like everything is falling apart or anything, but it has started causing problems.

At work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, like, “Did you send that?” or “Can you confirm this?” And I know they’re just trying to keep things moving, but I hate that feeling. It makes me feel like I’m becoming the person who can’t be trusted with the planning or the small details.

And at home it’s similar. If there’s something to organize, or plans to coordinate, I’ll mean to handle it, and then someone has to remind me. Then it becomes this whole tense thing, even if it started as something small. So yeah, I’d say it’s creating friction in both places, even though I really am trying.

Like, I can sit down to review a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and thinking about, like, the grocery list, or whether I replied to an email, or some random thing from three days ago.\n\nIt happens with personal stuff too. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, I can remember it pretty clearly from elementary school. Teachers would move my desk sometimes, or remind me to stay seated, because I was always kind of popping up or talking before I was called on.

And I’d rush through assignments too, even when I actually understood the work. Like, I wasn’t trying to be difficult… I just remember being sort of unable to slow down. It’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in years—like where my desk was in the classroom and how embarrassing it felt when they moved it closer to the teacher. So, yeah, I guess it has been there for a long time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the internal urge to move, honestly. Like, sometimes I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building up, like I need to shift positions or switch tasks or click a pen or do something with my hands.

It’s hard to explain… not just “I’m bored,” although sometimes I tell myself that. It’s more like if I don’t move or change something, I’m going to crawl out of my skin. And then my attention goes with that, because I’m spending so much energy trying to sit still.

Like, I wasn’t trying to be difficult… I just remember being sort of unable to slow down. It’s more like if I don’t move or change something, I’m going to crawl out of my skin. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it.

Like in meetings, especially with teammates, someone will be explaining a bug or asking a question, and I’ll start answering before they’ve actually finished. In my head I think I know where they’re going, so I jump in, and then sometimes I’m already talking when I realize, oh, wait, I guessed wrong.

It’s awkward because I don’t mean to cut people off. I’m usually trying to be helpful or move things along, but then I can see that I kind of interrupted the flow. I’ve tried to catch myself, but it still happens.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally made me come in is this thing where I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. Like during sprint planning at work, I’ll know I need to sit and pay attention, and then suddenly I’m up getting water or checking something in the other room.\n\nIt’s not just work either—on mortgage calls, which are already kind of stressful and boring, I’ll do the same thing. I’ll tell myself, “Just stay put,” and then two minutes later I’m wandering off for some random reason. It’s starting to feel noticeable, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're unable to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, like I might be sitting there looking calm, but inside it feels like this pressure building up—like I need to move, or switch to something else, or at least have something in my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just have to sit there and do nothing. And then I’ll start thinking about, I don’t know, the laundry or some random thing I forgot to check, and suddenly moving seems like the only way to make that feeling stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what you're doing when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’ll sit down to review a pull request and I genuinely intend to focus, like I’ll have the tab open and everything, but after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same block of code and my brain is off thinking about five totally unrelated things.\n\nIt happens with mortgage documents too, which are… honestly, no one enjoys those, but still. I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and then realize I haven’t absorbed any of it because I’m thinking about dinner or some message I forgot to answer. So yes, sitting still and focusing kind of fall apart together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that this restlessness and inability to focus happen more often when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think stress probably makes it louder, but the restlessness is kind of there even when I’m not, like, actively upset. I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside there’s this pressure, like I need to move or switch tasks or have something in my hands.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one meeting where I had a pen and kept clicking it until someone looked at me, and then I felt ridiculous and stopped, but then I just started bouncing my foot instead. So… yes, stress doesn’t help, but even on normal days, sitting still can feel like I’m trying not to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember it as clearly as an adult looking back, obviously, but I do remember in elementary school teachers moving my desk sometimes, or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was that kid who’d pop up for no real reason, or start talking before I was called on. And I’d rush through work even if I actually understood it, which is funny because now I’m a software developer and rushing is, like, the worst possible habit when you’re trying not to break production—sorry, tangent.\n\nBut yes, as a kid, there were definitely comments about me not staying seated and kind of blurting things out or hurrying through stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to focus affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, a bit. Not like everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be. At work, people have to follow up with me on planning stuff or little coordination things, and I hate that because I don’t want to be the person everyone has to chase.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, like with family plans or things I said I’d help organize. I’ll mean to do it, and then someone checks in again, and I feel this immediate guilt, like, oh no, I’m becoming unreliable. Which is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing people off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your restlessness and inability to focus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped doing anything, exactly. It’s more like I’ve had to change how I handle certain day-to-day stuff, especially planning things.\n\nLike at work, if there’s coordination or scheduling or some little follow-up task, people end up checking in with me more than they used to. And at home too, with family plans or organizing something, I’ll have good intentions and then someone has to remind me, and it creates friction.\n\nI really hate that feeling, because I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks. Like, not the person you want in charge of the details, which is… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus at home when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even with things I want to do. Like, I can sit down to review a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and thinking about, like, the grocery list, or whether I replied to an email, or some random thing from three days ago.\n\nIt happens with personal stuff too. We’ve been dealing with mortgage documents, and I’ll start reading them because, obviously, that’s important, and then I notice I’ve read the same paragraph four times and none of it has gone in. And then I get irritated with myself, because it’s not that I don’t care. My brain just sort of… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus consistently, regardless of the time of day or the activity you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not perfectly predictable by time of day, I don’t think. It can happen in different situations, even when I’m trying to be responsible.\n\nLike I’ll start reviewing a pull request with good intentions, or sit down with the mortgage documents because I know they matter, and after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same section. And my brain has gone off to five unrelated things, like some email I forgot or something on my grocery list. So it’s not only one specific activity, I guess. It kind of shows up across stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and inability to focus have caused significant problems in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “significant” sounds big, and it’s not like everything is falling apart or anything, but it has started causing problems.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, like, “Did you send that?” or “Can you confirm this?” And I know they’re just trying to keep things moving, but I hate that feeling. If there’s something to organize, or plans to coordinate, I’ll mean to handle it, and then someone has to remind me. Then it becomes this whole tense thing, even if it started as something small. And then I’ll start thinking about, I don’t know, the laundry or some random thing I forgot to check, and suddenly moving seems like the only way to make that feeling stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what you're doing when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’ll sit down to review a pull request and I genuinely intend to focus, like I’ll have the tab open and everything, but after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same block of code and my brain is off thinking about five totally unrelated things.\n\nIt happens with mortgage documents too, which are… honestly, no one enjoys those, but still. I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside there’s this pressure, like I need to move or switch tasks or have something in my hands.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one meeting where I had a pen and kept clicking it until someone looked at me, and then I felt ridiculous and stopped, but then I just started bouncing my foot instead. So… yes, stress doesn’t help, but even on normal days, sitting still can feel like I’m trying not to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember it as clearly as an adult looking back, obviously, but I do remember in elementary school teachers moving my desk sometimes, or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was that kid who’d pop up for no real reason, or start talking before I was called on. And I’d rush through work even if I actually understood it, which is funny because now I’m a software developer and rushing is, like, the worst possible habit when you’re trying not to break production—sorry, tangent.\n\nBut yes, as a kid, there were definitely comments about me not staying seated and kind of blurting things out or hurrying through stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to focus affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, a bit. Not like everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be. At work, people have to follow up with me on planning stuff or little coordination things, and I hate that because I don’t want to be the person everyone has to chase.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, like with family plans or things I said I’d help organize. I’ll mean to do it, and then someone checks in again, and I feel this immediate guilt, like, oh no, I’m becoming unreliable. Which is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing people off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your restlessness and inability to focus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped doing anything, exactly. It’s more like I’ve had to change how I handle certain day-to-day stuff, especially planning things.\n\nLike at work, if there’s coordination or scheduling or some little follow-up task, people end up checking in with me more than they used to. And at home too, with family plans or organizing something, I’ll have good intentions and then someone has to remind me, and it creates friction.\n\nI really hate that feeling, because I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks. Like, not the person you want in charge of the details, which is… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus at home when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even with things I want to do. Like, I can sit down to review a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and thinking about, like, the grocery list, or whether I replied to an email, or some random thing from three days ago.\n\nIt happens with personal stuff too. We’ve been dealing with mortgage documents, and I’ll start reading them because, obviously, that’s important, and then I notice I’ve read the same paragraph four times and none of it has gone in. And then I get irritated with myself, because it’s not that I don’t care. My brain just sort of… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus consistently, regardless of the time of day or the activity you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not perfectly predictable by time of day, I don’t think. It can happen in different situations, even when I’m trying to be responsible.\n\nLike I’ll start reviewing a pull request with good intentions, or sit down with the mortgage documents because I know they matter, and after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same section. And my brain has gone off to five unrelated things, like some email I forgot or something on my grocery list. So it’s not only one specific activity, I guess. It kind of shows up across stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and inability to focus have caused significant problems in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “significant” sounds big, and it’s not like everything is falling apart or anything, but it has started causing problems.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, like, “Did you send that?” or “Can you confirm this?” And I know they’re just trying to keep things moving, but I hate that feeling. It makes me feel like I’m becoming the person who can’t be trusted with the planning or the small details.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If there’s something to organize, or plans to coordinate, I’ll mean to handle it, and then someone has to remind me. Then it becomes this whole tense thing, even if it started as something small. And I’d rush through work even if I actually understood it, which is funny because now I’m a software developer and rushing is, like, the worst possible habit when you’re trying not to break production—sorry, tangent.\n\nBut yes, as a kid, there were definitely comments about me not staying seated and kind of blurting things out or hurrying through stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to focus affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, a bit. Not like everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be. At work, people have to follow up with me on planning stuff or little coordination things, and I hate that because I don’t want to be the person everyone has to chase.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, like with family plans or things I said I’d help organize. I’ll mean to do it, and then someone checks in again, and I feel this immediate guilt, like, oh no, I’m becoming unreliable. Which is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing people off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your restlessness and inability to focus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped doing anything, exactly. It’s more like I’ve had to change how I handle certain day-to-day stuff, especially planning things.\n\nLike at work, if there’s coordination or scheduling or some little follow-up task, people end up checking in with me more than they used to. And at home too, with family plans or organizing something, I’ll have good intentions and then someone has to remind me, and it creates friction.\n\nI really hate that feeling, because I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks. Like, not the person you want in charge of the details, which is… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus at home when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even with things I want to do. Like, I can sit down to review a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and thinking about, like, the grocery list, or whether I replied to an email, or some random thing from three days ago.\n\nIt happens with personal stuff too. We’ve been dealing with mortgage documents, and I’ll start reading them because, obviously, that’s important, and then I notice I’ve read the same paragraph four times and none of it has gone in. And then I get irritated with myself, because it’s not that I don’t care. My brain just sort of… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus consistently, regardless of the time of day or the activity you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not perfectly predictable by time of day, I don’t think. It can happen in different situations, even when I’m trying to be responsible.\n\nLike I’ll start reviewing a pull request with good intentions, or sit down with the mortgage documents because I know they matter, and after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same section. And my brain has gone off to five unrelated things, like some email I forgot or something on my grocery list. So it’s not only one specific activity, I guess. It kind of shows up across stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and inability to focus have caused significant problems in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “significant” sounds big, and it’s not like everything is falling apart or anything, but it has started causing problems.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, like, “Did you send that?” or “Can you confirm this?” And I know they’re just trying to keep things moving, but I hate that feeling. It makes me feel like I’m becoming the person who can’t be trusted with the planning or the small details.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If there’s something to organize, or plans to coordinate, I’ll mean to handle it, and then someone has to remind me. Then it becomes this whole tense thing, even if it started as something small. So yeah, I’d say it’s creating friction in both places, even though I really am trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus consistently throughout your life, since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, I can remember it pretty clearly from elementary school. Teachers would move my desk sometimes, or remind me to stay seated, because I was always kind of popping up or talking before I was called on.\n\nAnd I’d rush through assignments too, even when I actually understood the work. Like, I wasn’t trying to be difficult… I just remember being sort of unable to slow down. It’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in years—like where my desk was in the classroom and how embarrassing it felt when they moved it closer to the teacher. So, yeah, I guess it has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and focusing is more related to not being able to pay attention or to feeling an internal urge to move and be active?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the internal urge to move, honestly. Like, sometimes I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building up, like I need to shift positions or switch tasks or click a pen or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain… not just “I’m bored,” although sometimes I tell myself that. It’s more like if I don’t move or change something, I’m going to crawl out of my skin. And then my attention goes with that, because I’m spending so much energy trying to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike in meetings, especially with teammates, someone will be explaining a bug or asking a question, and I’ll start answering before they’ve actually finished. In my head I think I know where they’re going, so I jump in, and then sometimes I’m already talking when I realize, oh, wait, I guessed wrong.\n\nIt’s awkward because I don’t mean to cut people off. I’m usually trying to be helpful or move things along, but then I can see that I kind of interrupted the flow. I’ve tried to catch myself, but it still happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't pay attention or that you feel a strong need to be active and move around?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I need to be active or do something, yeah. I mean, I can be sitting there looking calm enough, like in a meeting or even just at my desk, but inside it’s like this pressure building.\n\nI’ll start shifting around, or wanting to switch tasks, or I need something in my hands—like a pen or whatever—because otherwise I get that crawly feeling, like I’m going to jump out of my skin. And then paying attention gets harder because I’m using all this effort just to not move.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, I think it has, though maybe not in a total falling-apart way. It’s more like… the regular stuff takes more managing than it should.

I mean, I’ll sit down to review a pull request and I genuinely intend to focus, like I’ll have the tab open and everything, but after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same block of code and my brain is off thinking about five totally unrelated things.\n\nIt happens with mortgage documents too, which are… honestly, no one enjoys those, but still. I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and then realize I haven’t absorbed any of it because I’m thinking about dinner or some message I forgot to answer. So yes, sitting still and focusing kind of fall apart together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that this restlessness and inability to focus happen more often when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think stress probably makes it louder, but the restlessness is kind of there even when I’m not, like, actively upset. I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside there’s this pressure, like I need to move or switch tasks or have something in my hands.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one meeting where I had a pen and kept clicking it until someone looked at me, and then I felt ridiculous and stopped, but then I just started bouncing my foot instead. So… yes, stress doesn’t help, but even on normal days, sitting still can feel like I’m trying not to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember it as clearly as an adult looking back, obviously, but I do remember in elementary school teachers moving my desk sometimes, or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was that kid who’d pop up for no real reason, or start talking before I was called on. And I’d rush through work even if I actually understood it, which is funny because now I’m a software developer and rushing is, like, the worst possible habit when you’re trying not to break production—sorry, tangent.\n\nBut yes, as a kid, there were definitely comments about me not staying seated and kind of blurting things out or hurrying through stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to focus affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, a bit. Not like everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be. At work, people have to follow up with me on planning stuff or little coordination things, and I hate that because I don’t want to be the person everyone has to chase.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, like with family plans or things I said I’d help organize. I’ll mean to do it, and then someone checks in again, and I feel this immediate guilt, like, oh no, I’m becoming unreliable. Which is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing people off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your restlessness and inability to focus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped doing anything, exactly. It’s more like I’ve had to change how I handle certain day-to-day stuff, especially planning things.\n\nLike at work, if there’s coordination or scheduling or some little follow-up task, people end up checking in with me more than they used to. And at home too, with family plans or organizing something, I’ll have good intentions and then someone has to remind me, and it creates friction.\n\nI really hate that feeling, because I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks. Like, not the person you want in charge of the details, which is… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus at home when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even with things I want to do. Like, I can sit down to review a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and thinking about, like, the grocery list, or whether I replied to an email, or some random thing from three days ago.\n\nIt happens with personal stuff too. We’ve been dealing with mortgage documents, and I’ll start reading them because, obviously, that’s important, and then I notice I’ve read the same paragraph four times and none of it has gone in. And then I get irritated with myself, because it’s not that I don’t care. My brain just sort of… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus consistently, regardless of the time of day or the activity you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not perfectly predictable by time of day, I don’t think. It can happen in different situations, even when I’m trying to be responsible.\n\nLike I’ll start reviewing a pull request with good intentions, or sit down with the mortgage documents because I know they matter, and after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same section. And my brain has gone off to five unrelated things, like some email I forgot or something on my grocery list. So it’s not only one specific activity, I guess. It kind of shows up across stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and inability to focus have caused significant problems in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “significant” sounds big, and it’s not like everything is falling apart or anything, but it has started causing problems.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, like, “Did you send that?” or “Can you confirm this?” And I know they’re just trying to keep things moving, but I hate that feeling. It makes me feel like I’m becoming the person who can’t be trusted with the planning or the small details.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If there’s something to organize, or plans to coordinate, I’ll mean to handle it, and then someone has to remind me. Then it becomes this whole tense thing, even if it started as something small. So yeah, I’d say it’s creating friction in both places, even though I really am trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus consistently throughout your life, since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, I can remember it pretty clearly from elementary school. Teachers would move my desk sometimes, or remind me to stay seated, because I was always kind of popping up or talking before I was called on.\n\nAnd I’d rush through assignments too, even when I actually understood the work. Like, I wasn’t trying to be difficult… I just remember being sort of unable to slow down. It’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in years—like where my desk was in the classroom and how embarrassing it felt when they moved it closer to the teacher. So, yeah, I guess it has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and focusing is more related to not being able to pay attention or to feeling an internal urge to move and be active?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the internal urge to move, honestly. Like, sometimes I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building up, like I need to shift positions or switch tasks or click a pen or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain… not just “I’m bored,” although sometimes I tell myself that. It’s more like if I don’t move or change something, I’m going to crawl out of my skin. And then my attention goes with that, because I’m spending so much energy trying to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike in meetings, especially with teammates, someone will be explaining a bug or asking a question, and I’ll start answering before they’ve actually finished. In my head I think I know where they’re going, so I jump in, and then sometimes I’m already talking when I realize, oh, wait, I guessed wrong.\n\nIt’s awkward because I don’t mean to cut people off. I’m usually trying to be helpful or move things along, but then I can see that I kind of interrupted the flow. I’ve tried to catch myself, but it still happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't pay attention or that you feel a strong need to be active and move around?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I need to be active or do something, yeah. I mean, I can be sitting there looking calm enough, like in a meeting or even just at my desk, but inside it’s like this pressure building.\n\nI’ll start shifting around, or wanting to switch tasks, or I need something in my hands—like a pen or whatever—because otherwise I get that crawly feeling, like I’m going to jump out of my skin. And then paying attention gets harder because I’m using all this effort just to not move.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to someone close to you, like a family member or friend, expressing concern about your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, not in a dramatic “we need to sit you down” kind of way, but people close to me have started pointing it out.\n\nAt home it’s more like, “Did you do that thing?” or “Can you please not forget this one?” And at work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, which… I hate admitting that. I used to feel like I was pretty dependable with planning and keeping things moving.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling it. But yes, there’s been more friction, both at home and work, because people are checking behind me or reminding me, and I worry I’m turning into the unreliable one for planning stuff even though I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain a regular routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has, though maybe not in a total falling-apart way. It’s more like… the regular stuff takes more managing than it should.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, and that’s embarrassing because I used to be the one who was pretty on top of planning things. And at home it’s similar—little reminders, checking whether I did what I said I’d do. It creates this friction, like I can tell people are trying not to be annoyed, but they kind of are.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s affecting my routine enough that I’m worried I’m becoming the unreliable one, even though I’m really trying hard not to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  },

I’m not really sure. I haven’t had, like, a clear answer about it before, which is part of why I’m here.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally made me come in is this thing where I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. Like during sprint planning at work, I’ll know I need to sit and pay attention, and then suddenly I’m up getting water or checking something in the other room.\n\nIt’s not just work either—on mortgage calls, which are already kind of stressful and boring, I’ll do the same thing. I’ll tell myself, “Just stay put,” and then two minutes later I’m wandering off for some random reason. It’s starting to feel noticeable, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're unable to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, like I might be sitting there looking calm, but inside it feels like this pressure building up—like I need to move, or switch to something else, or at least have something in my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just have to sit there and do nothing. And then I’ll start thinking about, I don’t know, the laundry or some random thing I forgot to check, and suddenly moving seems like the only way to make that feeling stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what you're doing when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’ll sit down to review a pull request and I genuinely intend to focus, like I’ll have the tab open and everything, but after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same block of code and my brain is off thinking about five totally unrelated things.\n\nIt happens with mortgage documents too, which are… honestly, no one enjoys those, but still. I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and then realize I haven’t absorbed any of it because I’m thinking about dinner or some message I forgot to answer. So yes, sitting still and focusing kind of fall apart together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that this restlessness and inability to focus happen more often when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think stress probably makes it louder, but the restlessness is kind of there even when I’m not, like, actively upset. I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside there’s this pressure, like I need to move or switch tasks or have something in my hands.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one meeting where I had a pen and kept clicking it until someone looked at me, and then I felt ridiculous and stopped, but then I just started bouncing my foot instead. So… yes, stress doesn’t help, but even on normal days, sitting still can feel like I’m trying not to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember it as clearly as an adult looking back, obviously, but I do remember in elementary school teachers moving my desk sometimes, or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was that kid who’d pop up for no real reason, or start talking before I was called on. And I’d rush through work even if I actually understood it, which is funny because now I’m a software developer and rushing is, like, the worst possible habit when you’re trying not to break production—sorry, tangent.\n\nBut yes, as a kid, there were definitely comments about me not staying seated and kind of blurting things out or hurrying through stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to focus affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, a bit. Not like everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be. At work, people have to follow up with me on planning stuff or little coordination things, and I hate that because I don’t want to be the person everyone has to chase.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, like with family plans or things I said I’d help organize. I’ll mean to do it, and then someone checks in again, and I feel this immediate guilt, like, oh no, I’m becoming unreliable. Which is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing people off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your restlessness and inability to focus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped doing anything, exactly. It’s more like I’ve had to change how I handle certain day-to-day stuff, especially planning things.\n\nLike at work, if there’s coordination or scheduling or some little follow-up task, people end up checking in with me more than they used to. And at home too, with family plans or organizing something, I’ll have good intentions and then someone has to remind me, and it creates friction.\n\nI really hate that feeling, because I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks. Like, not the person you want in charge of the details, which is… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus at home when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even with things I want to do. Like, I can sit down to review a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and thinking about, like, the grocery list, or whether I replied to an email, or some random thing from three days ago.\n\nIt happens with personal stuff too. We’ve been dealing with mortgage documents, and I’ll start reading them because, obviously, that’s important, and then I notice I’ve read the same paragraph four times and none of it has gone in. And then I get irritated with myself, because it’s not that I don’t care. My brain just sort of… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus consistently, regardless of the time of day or the activity you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not perfectly predictable by time of day, I don’t think. It can happen in different situations, even when I’m trying to be responsible.\n\nLike I’ll start reviewing a pull request with good intentions, or sit down with the mortgage documents because I know they matter, and after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same section. And my brain has gone off to five unrelated things, like some email I forgot or something on my grocery list. So it’s not only one specific activity, I guess. It kind of shows up across stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and inability to focus have caused significant problems in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “significant” sounds big, and it’s not like everything is falling apart or anything, but it has started causing problems.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, like, “Did you send that?” or “Can you confirm this?” And I know they’re just trying to keep things moving, but I hate that feeling. It makes me feel like I’m becoming the person who can’t be trusted with the planning or the small details.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If there’s something to organize, or plans to coordinate, I’ll mean to handle it, and then someone has to remind me. Then it becomes this whole tense thing, even if it started as something small. So yeah, I’d say it’s creating friction in both places, even though I really am trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus consistently throughout your life, since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, I can remember it pretty clearly from elementary school. Teachers would move my desk sometimes, or remind me to stay seated, because I was always kind of popping up or talking before I was called on.\n\nAnd I’d rush through assignments too, even when I actually understood the work. Like, I wasn’t trying to be difficult… I just remember being sort of unable to slow down. It’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in years—like where my desk was in the classroom and how embarrassing it felt when they moved it closer to the teacher. So, yeah, I guess it has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and focusing is more related to not being able to pay attention or to feeling an internal urge to move and be active?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the internal urge to move, honestly. Like, sometimes I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building up, like I need to shift positions or switch tasks or click a pen or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain… not just “I’m bored,” although sometimes I tell myself that. It’s more like if I don’t move or change something, I’m going to crawl out of my skin. And then my attention goes with that, because I’m spending so much energy trying to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike in meetings, especially with teammates, someone will be explaining a bug or asking a question, and I’ll start answering before they’ve actually finished. In my head I think I know where they’re going, so I jump in, and then sometimes I’m already talking when I realize, oh, wait, I guessed wrong.\n\nIt’s awkward because I don’t mean to cut people off. I’m usually trying to be helpful or move things along, but then I can see that I kind of interrupted the flow. I’ve tried to catch myself, but it still happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't pay attention or that you feel a strong need to be active and move around?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I need to be active or do something, yeah. I mean, I can be sitting there looking calm enough, like in a meeting or even just at my desk, but inside it’s like this pressure building.\n\nI’ll start shifting around, or wanting to switch tasks, or I need something in my hands—like a pen or whatever—because otherwise I get that crawly feeling, like I’m going to jump out of my skin. And then paying attention gets harder because I’m using all this effort just to not move.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to someone close to you, like a family member or friend, expressing concern about your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. But yes, it’s affecting my routine enough that I’m worried I’m becoming the unreliable one, even though I’m really trying hard not to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder or do you think you might have it?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had, like, a clear answer about it before, which is part of why I’m here.\n\nI’ve wondered about it, but then I also second-guess myself because everyone jokes about being “a little like that” now, especially in tech, and it’s hard to tell what’s normal stress versus something else. So… I guess the honest answer is I don’t know, and I’m trying not to self-diagnose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I think… more on the hyperactive side, maybe? Not like I’m literally running around or anything. From the outside I can look like I’m sitting calmly, especially at work, but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building—like I need to move, or switch tasks, or pick something up and do something with my hands.

It’s starting to feel noticeable, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're unable to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, like I might be sitting there looking calm, but inside it feels like this pressure building up—like I need to move, or switch to something else, or at least have something in my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just have to sit there and do nothing. And then I’ll start thinking about, I don’t know, the laundry or some random thing I forgot to check, and suddenly moving seems like the only way to make that feeling stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what you're doing when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’ll sit down to review a pull request and I genuinely intend to focus, like I’ll have the tab open and everything, but after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same block of code and my brain is off thinking about five totally unrelated things.\n\nIt happens with mortgage documents too, which are… honestly, no one enjoys those, but still. I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and then realize I haven’t absorbed any of it because I’m thinking about dinner or some message I forgot to answer. So yes, sitting still and focusing kind of fall apart together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that this restlessness and inability to focus happen more often when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think stress probably makes it louder, but the restlessness is kind of there even when I’m not, like, actively upset. I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside there’s this pressure, like I need to move or switch tasks or have something in my hands.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one meeting where I had a pen and kept clicking it until someone looked at me, and then I felt ridiculous and stopped, but then I just started bouncing my foot instead. So… yes, stress doesn’t help, but even on normal days, sitting still can feel like I’m trying not to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember it as clearly as an adult looking back, obviously, but I do remember in elementary school teachers moving my desk sometimes, or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was that kid who’d pop up for no real reason, or start talking before I was called on. And I’d rush through work even if I actually understood it, which is funny because now I’m a software developer and rushing is, like, the worst possible habit when you’re trying not to break production—sorry, tangent.\n\nBut yes, as a kid, there were definitely comments about me not staying seated and kind of blurting things out or hurrying through stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to focus affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, a bit. Not like everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be. At work, people have to follow up with me on planning stuff or little coordination things, and I hate that because I don’t want to be the person everyone has to chase.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, like with family plans or things I said I’d help organize. I’ll mean to do it, and then someone checks in again, and I feel this immediate guilt, like, oh no, I’m becoming unreliable. Which is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing people off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your restlessness and inability to focus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped doing anything, exactly. It’s more like I’ve had to change how I handle certain day-to-day stuff, especially planning things.\n\nLike at work, if there’s coordination or scheduling or some little follow-up task, people end up checking in with me more than they used to. And at home too, with family plans or organizing something, I’ll have good intentions and then someone has to remind me, and it creates friction.\n\nI really hate that feeling, because I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks. Like, not the person you want in charge of the details, which is… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus at home when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even with things I want to do. Like, I can sit down to review a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and thinking about, like, the grocery list, or whether I replied to an email, or some random thing from three days ago.\n\nIt happens with personal stuff too. We’ve been dealing with mortgage documents, and I’ll start reading them because, obviously, that’s important, and then I notice I’ve read the same paragraph four times and none of it has gone in. And then I get irritated with myself, because it’s not that I don’t care. My brain just sort of… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus consistently, regardless of the time of day or the activity you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not perfectly predictable by time of day, I don’t think. It can happen in different situations, even when I’m trying to be responsible.\n\nLike I’ll start reviewing a pull request with good intentions, or sit down with the mortgage documents because I know they matter, and after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same section. And my brain has gone off to five unrelated things, like some email I forgot or something on my grocery list. So it’s not only one specific activity, I guess. It kind of shows up across stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and inability to focus have caused significant problems in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “significant” sounds big, and it’s not like everything is falling apart or anything, but it has started causing problems.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, like, “Did you send that?” or “Can you confirm this?” And I know they’re just trying to keep things moving, but I hate that feeling. It makes me feel like I’m becoming the person who can’t be trusted with the planning or the small details.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If there’s something to organize, or plans to coordinate, I’ll mean to handle it, and then someone has to remind me. Then it becomes this whole tense thing, even if it started as something small. So yeah, I’d say it’s creating friction in both places, even though I really am trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus consistently throughout your life, since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, I can remember it pretty clearly from elementary school. Teachers would move my desk sometimes, or remind me to stay seated, because I was always kind of popping up or talking before I was called on.\n\nAnd I’d rush through assignments too, even when I actually understood the work. Like, I wasn’t trying to be difficult… I just remember being sort of unable to slow down. It’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in years—like where my desk was in the classroom and how embarrassing it felt when they moved it closer to the teacher. So, yeah, I guess it has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and focusing is more related to not being able to pay attention or to feeling an internal urge to move and be active?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the internal urge to move, honestly. Like, sometimes I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building up, like I need to shift positions or switch tasks or click a pen or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain… not just “I’m bored,” although sometimes I tell myself that. It’s more like if I don’t move or change something, I’m going to crawl out of my skin. And then my attention goes with that, because I’m spending so much energy trying to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike in meetings, especially with teammates, someone will be explaining a bug or asking a question, and I’ll start answering before they’ve actually finished. In my head I think I know where they’re going, so I jump in, and then sometimes I’m already talking when I realize, oh, wait, I guessed wrong.\n\nIt’s awkward because I don’t mean to cut people off. I’m usually trying to be helpful or move things along, but then I can see that I kind of interrupted the flow. I’ve tried to catch myself, but it still happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't pay attention or that you feel a strong need to be active and move around?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I need to be active or do something, yeah. I mean, I can be sitting there looking calm enough, like in a meeting or even just at my desk, but inside it’s like this pressure building.\n\nI’ll start shifting around, or wanting to switch tasks, or I need something in my hands—like a pen or whatever—because otherwise I get that crawly feeling, like I’m going to jump out of my skin. And then paying attention gets harder because I’m using all this effort just to not move.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to someone close to you, like a family member or friend, expressing concern about your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. I mean, not in a dramatic “we need to sit you down” kind of way, but people close to me have started pointing it out.\n\nAt home it’s more like, “Did you do that thing?” or “Can you please not forget this one?” And at work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, which… I hate admitting that. I used to feel like I was pretty dependable with planning and keeping things moving.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling it. But yes, there’s been more friction, both at home and work, because people are checking behind me or reminding me, and I worry I’m turning into the unreliable one for planning stuff even though I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain a regular routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has, though maybe not in a total falling-apart way. It’s more like… the regular stuff takes more managing than it should.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, and that’s embarrassing because I used to be the one who was pretty on top of planning things. And at home it’s similar—little reminders, checking whether I did what I said I’d do. It creates this friction, like I can tell people are trying not to be annoyed, but they kind of are.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s affecting my routine enough that I’m worried I’m becoming the unreliable one, even though I’m really trying hard not to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder or do you think you might have it?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had, like, a clear answer about it before, which is part of why I’m here.\n\nI’ve wondered about it, but then I also second-guess myself because everyone jokes about being “a little like that” now, especially in tech, and it’s hard to tell what’s normal stress versus something else. So… I guess the honest answer is I don’t know, and I’m trying not to self-diagnose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or in other situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it sounds kind of childish when I say it out loud.\n\nLike in work stand-up, if I know what I want to say, waiting for the right moment makes me weirdly tense, and sometimes I jump in too fast. Then afterward I’m like, okay, that probably came off rude or impatient. And sitting on hold with the bank or anything like that—I’ll keep checking the time, switching screens, getting irritated, even if it’s only been a few minutes.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one time with customer service where I basically worked myself up over nothing—but yes, I do have trouble waiting my turn sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms are more related to inattention, hyperactivity, or a combination of both?\n\nPatient: I think… more on the hyperactive side, maybe? Not like I’m literally running around or anything. From the outside I can look like I’m sitting calmly, especially at work, but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building—like I need to move, or switch tasks, or pick something up and do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like if I just sit still and stay with one thing too long, I start feeling like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll be in a meeting looking normal, and meanwhile I’m thinking about needing to click a pen or change windows or stand up—sorry, I know that’s not exactly answering neatly.\n\nSo yeah, I guess if I had to choose, I’d say the restless, driven-to-move part feels more obvious to me than just not paying attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s not always obvious from the outside, like I might be sitting there looking calm, but inside it feels like this pressure building up—like I need to move, or switch to something else, or at least have something in my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just have to sit there and do nothing. And then I’ll start thinking about, I don’t know, the laundry or some random thing I forgot to check, and suddenly moving seems like the only way to make that feeling stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what you're doing when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’ll sit down to review a pull request and I genuinely intend to focus, like I’ll have the tab open and everything, but after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same block of code and my brain is off thinking about five totally unrelated things.\n\nIt happens with mortgage documents too, which are… honestly, no one enjoys those, but still. I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and then realize I haven’t absorbed any of it because I’m thinking about dinner or some message I forgot to answer. So yes, sitting still and focusing kind of fall apart together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that this restlessness and inability to focus happen more often when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think stress probably makes it louder, but the restlessness is kind of there even when I’m not, like, actively upset. I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside there’s this pressure, like I need to move or switch tasks or have something in my hands.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one meeting where I had a pen and kept clicking it until someone looked at me, and then I felt ridiculous and stopped, but then I just started bouncing my foot instead. So… yes, stress doesn’t help, but even on normal days, sitting still can feel like I’m trying not to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember it as clearly as an adult looking back, obviously, but I do remember in elementary school teachers moving my desk sometimes, or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was that kid who’d pop up for no real reason, or start talking before I was called on. And I’d rush through work even if I actually understood it, which is funny because now I’m a software developer and rushing is, like, the worst possible habit when you’re trying not to break production—sorry, tangent.\n\nBut yes, as a kid, there were definitely comments about me not staying seated and kind of blurting things out or hurrying through stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to focus affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, a bit. Not like everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be. At work, people have to follow up with me on planning stuff or little coordination things, and I hate that because I don’t want to be the person everyone has to chase.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, like with family plans or things I said I’d help organize. I’ll mean to do it, and then someone checks in again, and I feel this immediate guilt, like, oh no, I’m becoming unreliable. Which is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing people off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your restlessness and inability to focus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped doing anything, exactly. It’s more like I’ve had to change how I handle certain day-to-day stuff, especially planning things.\n\nLike at work, if there’s coordination or scheduling or some little follow-up task, people end up checking in with me more than they used to. And at home too, with family plans or organizing something, I’ll have good intentions and then someone has to remind me, and it creates friction.\n\nI really hate that feeling, because I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks. Like, not the person you want in charge of the details, which is… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus at home when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even with things I want to do. Like, I can sit down to review a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and thinking about, like, the grocery list, or whether I replied to an email, or some random thing from three days ago.\n\nIt happens with personal stuff too. We’ve been dealing with mortgage documents, and I’ll start reading them because, obviously, that’s important, and then I notice I’ve read the same paragraph four times and none of it has gone in. And then I get irritated with myself, because it’s not that I don’t care. My brain just sort of… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and inability to focus consistently, regardless of the time of day or the activity you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not perfectly predictable by time of day, I don’t think. It can happen in different situations, even when I’m trying to be responsible.\n\nLike I’ll start reviewing a pull request with good intentions, or sit down with the mortgage documents because I know they matter, and after a few minutes I realize I’m just staring at the same section. And my brain has gone off to five unrelated things, like some email I forgot or something on my grocery list. So it’s not only one specific activity, I guess. It kind of shows up across stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness and inability to focus have caused significant problems in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “significant” sounds big, and it’s not like everything is falling apart or anything, but it has started causing problems.\n\nAt work, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, like, “Did you send that?” or “Can you confirm this?” And I know they’re just trying to keep things moving, but I hate that feeling. It makes me feel like I’m becoming the person who can’t be trusted with the planning or the small details.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If there’s something to organize, or plans to coordinate, I’ll mean to handle it, and then someone has to remind me. Then it becomes this whole tense thing, even if it started as something small. So yeah, I’d say it’s creating friction in both places, even though I really am trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and inability to focus consistently throughout your life, since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, looking back, I can remember it pretty clearly from elementary school. Teachers would move my desk sometimes, or remind me to stay seated, because I was always kind of popping up or talking before I was called on.\n\nAnd I’d rush through assignments too, even when I actually understood the work. Like, I wasn’t trying to be difficult… I just remember being sort of unable to slow down. It’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in years—like where my desk was in the classroom and how embarrassing it felt when they moved it closer to the teacher. So, yeah, I guess it has been there for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and focusing is more related to not being able to pay attention or to feeling an internal urge to move and be active?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the internal urge to move, honestly. Like, sometimes I can look totally calm sitting there, but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building up, like I need to shift positions or switch tasks or click a pen or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain… not just “I’m bored,” although sometimes I tell myself that. It’s more like if I don’t move or change something, I’m going to crawl out of my skin. And then my attention goes with that, because I’m spending so much energy trying to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not constantly, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike in meetings, especially with teammates, someone will be explaining a bug or asking a question, and I’ll start answering before they’ve actually finished. In my head I think I know where they’re going, so I jump in, and then sometimes I’m already talking when I realize, oh, wait, I guessed wrong.\n\nIt’s awkward because I don’t mean to cut people off. I’m usually trying to be helpful or move things along, but then I can see that I kind of interrupted the flow. I’ve tried to catch myself, but it still happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't pay attention or that you feel a strong need to be active and move around?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I need to be active or do something, yeah. I mean, I can be sitting there looking calm enough, like in a meeting or even just at my desk, but inside it’s like this pressure building.\n\nI’ll start shifting around, or wanting to switch tasks, or I need something in my hands—like a pen or whatever—because otherwise I get that crawly feeling, like I’m going to jump out of my skin. It creates this friction, like I can tell people are trying not to be annoyed, but they kind of are.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering a bit. But yes, it’s affecting my routine enough that I’m worried I’m becoming the unreliable one, even though I’m really trying hard not to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder or do you think you might have it?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had, like, a clear answer about it before, which is part of why I’m here.\n\nI’ve wondered about it, but then I also second-guess myself because everyone jokes about being “a little like that” now, especially in tech, and it’s hard to tell what’s normal stress versus something else. So… I guess the honest answer is I don’t know, and I’m trying not to self-diagnose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or in other situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but it sounds kind of childish when I say it out loud.\n\nLike in work stand-up, if I know what I want to say, waiting for the right moment makes me weirdly tense, and sometimes I jump in too fast. Then afterward I’m like, okay, that probably came off rude or impatient. And sitting on hold with the bank or anything like that—I’ll keep checking the time, switching screens, getting irritated, even if it’s only been a few minutes.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one time with customer service where I basically worked myself up over nothing—but yes, I do have trouble waiting my turn sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms are more related to inattention, hyperactivity, or a combination of both?\n\nPatient: I think… more on the hyperactive side, maybe? I’ll be in a meeting looking normal, and meanwhile I’m thinking about needing to click a pen or change windows or stand up—sorry, I know that’s not exactly answering neatly.\n\nSo yeah, I guess if I had to choose, I’d say the restless, driven-to-move part feels more obvious to me than just not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have physical restlessness, such as fidgeting or feeling a strong need to get up and move around, in quiet environments like libraries or movie theaters?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so.
